# MOI Product SDK Capability Plan

Status: Product SDKs share generated response contracts. Route mappings and capability-name checks do not establish full parameter or behavior alignment; verify against the current MOI handlers and HTTP protocol tests.

Availability: the consumer-memory/profile task and its `MemoryService` / `ConsumerMemoryHandle` entries below describe the legacy server only. Current MOI does not serve those routes. Do not choose that task for the current product; see [Legacy API availability](#legacy-api-availability). Finetune jobs are intentionally not a Product SDK or CLI surface; their backend routes and native acceptance remain owned by MOI.

Owner: MOI Product SDK

Repository boundary: `sdk/`

Current implementation:

- Human PAT callers use Go `sdk.NewWithPersonalAccessToken(productEndpoint, pat, ...)` or Python `sdk.new_with_personal_access_token(product_endpoint, pat, ...)`. Both send the API key authentication used by Product SDK callers.
- Go and Python expose workspace-scoped Current Role selection through `sdk.WithWorkspaceCurrentRoleID(workspaceID, roleID)` and `sdk.with_workspace_current_role_id(workspace_id, role_id)`. The CLI consumes the same selection. Role input is an untrusted candidate; the server remains authoritative for direct binding, lifecycle and the single Effective Role.
- Implemented route coverage includes workspace/account/RBAC, catalog/files, knowledge, workflow/runtime/lineage, agent A2A and workspace agent resource proxy routes, Explore sessions/messages, model/provider routing, connectors/import/export, SQL editor/query history, compute, data share.
- Anonymous Agent Webapp link/session, conversation, feedback, MCP App, A2A and artifact routes use `Client.Webapp` / `client.webapp`; the transport strips product API keys, bearer tokens, cookies, workspace and runtime grants instead of forwarding them to the public edge.
- Go and Python workspace bootstrap methods now expose typed product results for list/create/claim/users; update/delete/bind-user return explicit errors for OK-envelope writes.
- Go and Python account bootstrap methods now expose typed product results for detail, self-info update, API key preview/reveal/refresh, and DB connection.
- Go and Python audit log methods now expose typed product results for operation log list and overview.
- Go and Python catalog file methods now expose typed product results for upload/info/preview-link/trigger-workflow; create/update/delete/delete-ref return explicit errors for OK-envelope writes.
- Go and Python catalog folder/tag methods now expose typed product results for create/ref-list/list where the backend returns product data; update/delete/clean/create-tag return explicit errors for OK-envelope writes.
- Go and Python connector/import/export task methods now return explicit errors for backend OK-envelope writes that carry no product data, including connector update/delete, connector file delete, import task update/delete/pause/resume/retry-files, and export task delete/retry.
- Go and Python data-share nil-data writes now return explicit errors only for delete publish and unsubscribe.
- Go and Python member-management nil-data writes return explicit errors for supported member create/update/delete/status and canonical role-binding updates; legacy role, grant, object-permission, and privilege-check operations are not part of the Product SDK.
- Go and Python compute nil-data delete now returns explicit errors only while read/update/preflight/action methods keep their backend response data.
- Workspace, account bootstrap, catalog file, catalog common small-result, connector source database list, connector file download, data-share summary/name-check, and WorkItem API service contracts are defined in `sdk/proto/product/*.proto` and generated through `sdk/Makefile` into `sdk/go-sdk/product` and `sdk/python-sdk/moi_product_sdk/_proto`.
- `moi-backend` auth workspace/account, catalog file, and catalog folder/tag/ref-list response handlers reuse the generated product contract for those bootstrap responses instead of hand-written duplicate response DTOs.
- Non-app Go SDK methods outside explicit design exemptions no longer return `ProductData`; the hard maturity baseline tracks zero non-exempt findings.
- Agent resource routes are exposed as explicit product endpoint resource calls first. They remain `backend-proxy-to-core` until backend contracts are promoted into higher-level product operations.
- Audit log list operations use options-style helpers instead of accepting raw request bodies.
- The Apps backend domain, including `/newmoi/apps`, `/newmoi/apps/:app_code`, and all app-specific workflows, is not a Product SDK surface. Route manifests classify those backend routes as `not_applicable`; Go, Python, CLI, docs, and smoke expose no Apps API.
- CI source of truth for route coverage is the runtime Gin route manifest generated from production-like backend route registration, cross-checked against backend source scanning and SDK route mappings, not this document.
- Route coverage is not the same as SDK capability completion. The maturity target is zero `ProductData` responses, zero raw request bodies, zero raw responses, zero generic request/response containers, and zero generic proxy operations unless a method has an explicit Product SDK design exemption.

Current gaps:

- Non-app Go SDK public methods no longer return `RawMessage`; new non-exempt `ProductData` returns fail the maturity gate until promoted to typed product contracts.
- Remaining Go/Python raw request bodies and generic proxies are limited to the explicit agent workspace resource proxy design exemption.
- Apps backend routes are `not_applicable` to the Product SDK and must not appear in SDK route mappings or public interfaces.
- Integration coverage currently proves representative product endpoint and API-key flows, not every UI-visible capability.
- User-story facades make the workspace/catalog/database/table/volume/folder/file path convenient and AI-friendly. Member management uses `WorkspaceMemberHandle` for member detail/update/status/deletion and `IAMService` for canonical role-binding reads/replacements without repeating workspace/member ids; supported general audit access is `WorkspaceMemberService.AuditLogs`, not a member-scoped SDK method. Canonical role, policy, grant, and data-privilege administration remain Core-owned BFF contracts rather than Product SDK handles. Catalog browsing turns UI catalog/database/children results into scoped handles, and the other domain handles below retain their existing resource scope and behavior.
- Catalog tree node IDs preserve the backend `number|string` union as `google.protobuf.Value`; callers should inspect the Value kind instead of assuming all system catalog leaves have numeric ids.
- Model provider handles fail locally on missing required fields for LLM setup, backend creation, endpoint creation, and endpoint status changes before issuing HTTP requests.
- WorkItem API service publishing now has scoped handles and explicit publish/invoke specs, so callers can inspect, publish, enable, invoke, and disable one catalog WorkItem version without hand-building route bodies or repeating `node_id` and `version`.
- The next high-level abstractions should be driven by full user tasks rather than route groups: for example "manage an agent resource lifecycle" and "inspect agent runtime/task state from a user workflow". Each abstraction should have Go/Python parity plus one unit user story and one product endpoint integration test before being called complete. Workflow execution, connector task, provider backend, alert receiver, alert rule, compute resource, data-share publish/subscription, SQL workbook/version, SQL query, and SQL statement handles intentionally require known ids from the backend response, list, or UI state; the SDK does not infer those ids from untyped `ProductData`. Agent resource CRUD/runtime handles should not be added on top of the current `ANY` proxy routes until backend promotes stable product request/response contracts for the target user task. Explore message-list APIs require a numeric session id, while Explore query/session path APIs use a string id; the handle either strictly parses a positive integer string or accepts an explicit message-list id through `ExploreSessionWithMessageID` / `explore_session_with_message_id`.

Gate semantics:

The user-story facade alignment test is the high-level counterpart to route alignment. It requires every promoted task to have User Story Stability Evidence with real Go/Python facade tests, stable proof, and failure boundary. It checks that every task has User Story State Handoff Coverage covering carried typed state, public accessor/result source, next-call binding, forbidden handoff shortcut, and AI-safe handoff behavior; every task has User Story Discovery and Selection Coverage explaining how users or AI discover/create resources before binding handles from ids; every task has User Story Selection Disambiguation Coverage covering selection input, uniqueness rule, pagination/search boundary, ambiguous/missing selection handling, and forbidden selection shortcut; every task has User Story Ordering and Recency Coverage covering ordering intent, authoritative order source, recency/time-window source, forbidden ordering shortcut, and AI-safe ordering behavior; every task has a User Story Outcome Contract naming the user-visible result, continuation handle/spec, and caller-owned input boundary; each Outcome Contract user-visible result mentions a matching Flow Recipe product; every task has User Story Interaction Mode Coverage covering synchronous, long-running, streaming, pagination, batch, destructive, cancel, force, and approval behavior; every task has User Story Execution Budget Coverage covering timeout/deadline source, polling/stream stop condition, page/batch limit, retry budget, and approval/escalation boundary; every task has User Story Pagination and Partial Result Coverage covering paginated/partial surfaces, continuation token/source, complete-result proof, forbidden partial-result shortcut, and AI-safe pagination behavior; every task has User Story Error Recovery Coverage covering retryable failures, user-correctable failures, forbidden recovery, and AI-safe next action; every task has User Story Permission and Scope Coverage covering product scope, permission-sensitive operations, caller-owned authorization boundary, forbidden scope/RBAC behavior, and AI-safe permission handling; every task has User Story Ownership and Collaboration Coverage covering ownership evidence, collaborator/shared scope, caller-owned decision boundary, forbidden cross-user shortcut, and AI-safe collaboration behavior; every task has User Story Input Construction Coverage covering required caller inputs, derived typed-result inputs, option/enum/secret rules, forbidden construction behavior, and AI-safe construction; every task has User Story Value Format and Units Coverage covering value/format contract, unit/timezone authority, enum/identifier format, forbidden format shortcut, and AI-safe value behavior; every task has User Story Default and Omission Coverage covering optional omissions, invalid empty/zero values, backend/default authority, forbidden defaulting behavior, and AI-safe omission behavior; every task has User Story Completion Verification Coverage covering completion signal, verification call, async/delete/cancel handling, forbidden completion shortcut, and AI-safe stop condition; every task has User Story Result Accessor Coverage covering public result/accessor source, user-readable fields, continuation fields, forbidden extraction shortcut, and AI-safe accessor behavior; every task has User Story Output Consumption Coverage covering user-facing output, continuation output, sensitive/internal output boundary, forbidden output consumption, and AI-safe consumption behavior; every task has User Story Sensitive Data Handling Coverage covering sensitive inputs, sensitive outputs, logging/example boundary, persistence/AI-context boundary, and AI-safe handling; every task has User Story Automation Safety Coverage covering repeat-safe behavior, idempotency key or lookup, external/cost side-effect boundary, forbidden automation shortcut, and AI-safe automation behavior; every task has User Story Concurrent Submission Coverage covering operation identity, parallel-safe reads, serialized mutation boundary, forbidden duplicate submission, and AI-safe in-flight behavior; every task has User Story Impact Assessment Coverage covering impact source, affected scope, cost/external side effects, uncertainty boundary, and AI-safe impact summary; every task has User Story Confirmation and Preview Coverage covering confirmation trigger, preview/preflight source, user-visible confirmation summary, forbidden implicit approval, and AI-safe confirmation behavior; every task has User Story Progress Visibility Coverage covering progress signal, status/read call, user-visible update, forbidden silent wait, and AI-safe progress reporting; every task has User Story Diagnostic Handoff Coverage covering safe diagnostic context, correlation/status handle, sensitive diagnostic boundary, forbidden diagnostic shortcut, and AI-safe diagnostic handoff; and every task has User Story Freshness and Conflict Coverage covering fresh-state source, conflict/stale signal, destructive/update conflict boundary, forbidden stale-state shortcut, and AI-safe conflict handling.

AI Intent Disambiguation Matrix rows resolve overlapping intent before route/service selection. User Context Entry Matrix rows choose the SDK starting object from already available caller context. User Story Flow Recipe Start from entries must be supported by the User Context Entry Matrix or by a WorkspaceHandle/typed-result descendant. AI Task Selection Guide task order must match User Story Matrix order. Prefer entries must be supported by the matching Ergonomic Usage Tiers minimal or advanced-control path, and each task must have at least one Prefer entry supported by the minimal path. User Story Flow Recipes task order must match User Story Matrix order. User Story Outcome Contracts task order must match User Story Matrix order. All User task coverage tables must match User Story Matrix order. User Story Ergonomic Usage Tiers give every promoted task a minimal path, advanced-control path, and escape boundary. Ergonomic minimal paths must be backed by Flow Recipe products or Outcome Contract continuations; advanced-control code entries must be backed by the User Story Matrix, Flow Recipe, or Outcome Contract. User Story Operation Intent Coverage covers read/discover, create/configure, update/action, delete/cancel, and observe/result behavior for every promoted task. User Story Abstraction Fitness proves every promoted task has a user mental model, friendly handle/spec shape, hidden route/scope details, caller-owned boundary, and forbidden low-level shortcut. Task quick start task order must match User Story Matrix order. Task quick start entries must be supported by the matching Ergonomic Usage Tiers minimal or advanced-control path, and each task must have at least one quick-start entry supported by the minimal path. every task has User Story Representative Method Coverage naming visible Go/Python README calls. Representative call order is checked against matching code lines, not whole-block substrings. every task has User Story Example Scenario Coverage covering user scenario, copyable example anchor, inputs to replace, expected user-visible output, and AI adaptation boundary. Inputs to replace must mention a Flow Recipe or Outcome Contract source entry. Expected output must mention a Flow Recipe product already named by the matching Outcome Contract. It checks Cross-Domain Journey Example Anchors so multi-area outcomes point to real Go/Python README calls from the journey task sequence. Cross-Domain User Journey entry points for later tasks must be backed by produced handles/results from the same or earlier journey steps. It checks Cross-Domain Journey Risk Coverage so each multi-area outcome names failure/recovery, permission/governance, and external dependency boundaries.


The promoted user-story surface is backed by AI intent disambiguation, user context entry selection, ergonomic usage tiers, operation intent coverage, capability availability coverage, abstraction fitness, state handoff coverage, discovery/selection coverage, selection disambiguation coverage, ordering and recency coverage, outcome contracts, interaction mode coverage, execution budget coverage, pagination and partial result coverage, error recovery coverage, permission and scope coverage, ownership and collaboration coverage, input construction coverage, value format and units coverage, default and omission coverage, completion verification coverage, result accessor coverage, output consumption coverage, sensitive data handling coverage, automation safety coverage, concurrent submission coverage, impact assessment coverage, confirmation and preview coverage, abort and cleanup coverage, progress visibility coverage, diagnostic handoff coverage, freshness and conflict coverage, cross-domain example anchors, cross-domain risk coverage, stability evidence, and representative method coverage, example scenario coverage.

## Legacy API availability

The SDK still contains these Consumer Memory/Profile methods for existing callers. They require a legacy server that implements the routes; current MOI deliberately excludes this product contract, as recorded in the [current API checklist](../../moi/docs/current-api-checklist.md) and [migration scope](../../moi/docs/migration-test-coverage.md).

| Go / Python service method | Legacy route absent from current MOI |
| --- | --- |
| `Retrieve` / `retrieve` | `POST /newmoi/memories/retrieve` |
| `Search` / `search` | `POST /newmoi/memories/search` |
| `FulltextSearch` / `fulltext_search` | `POST /newmoi/memories/fulltext-search` |
| `Query` / `query` | `POST /newmoi/memories/query` |
| `RenderProfile` / `render_profile` | `GET /newmoi/consumer-profiles/{subject_id}/render` |

Go marks the legacy service and handle entry points deprecated; Python documents the same availability boundary. Their presence and local transport tests are not evidence of current native integration coverage. The current CLI has no Consumer Memory command domain. MOMO's conversation/runtime memory is a separate capability and does not implement these consumer endpoints. Existing legacy request behavior is retained.

## Workflow template controls

Go/Python template creation and updates accept the existing key, description, input-form and builtin options. CLI `create-workflow-template`, `create-mowl-workflow-template`, `update-workflow-template` and `update-mowl-workflow-template` expose them as `--template-key`, `--description`, `--input-form` and `--is-builtin`. Supplied `false` and empty strings are passed to the SDK unchanged; omitted flags do not create options.

`--input-form` takes a JSON-encoded **string**, sent as `runtime_fields`; a MOWL `--json` / `--json-file` takes the graph **object**. Use `moi-cli schema workflow update-mowl-workflow-template` for the graph input shape. Both MOWL commands use the same graph loader as deployment, and both SDKs share template option serialization between DSL and MOWL.

MOWL updates use Go `WorkflowService.UpdateMOWLWorkflowTemplate` / `WorkflowTemplateHandle.UpdateMOWL` and Python `update_mowl_workflow_template` / `WorkflowTemplateHandle.update_mowl`. The server validates custom operator references, rejects custom-to-builtin conversion with `ErrTemplateTypeImmutable`, and protects seed-managed builtin templates. Template updates replace the submitted definition and metadata; read the current template and supply description/form values when retaining them. The SDK does not invent preservation defaults.

Template response IDs use `uint64` in Go and the corresponding generated integer field in Python, matching the handler. Existing Go callers storing a template ID in `uint32` must use `uint64`. The shared `workflow_template_options_contract.json` checks DSL/MOWL requests, omission/false/empty values, owner error passthrough, and creation/readback IDs above the `uint32` range. It does not execute a template on a real backend.

## Workflow app authoring and versions

CLI `workflow update` exposes the existing SDK's `--dsl-yaml` and `--runtime-layout-file` inputs in addition to schedule, trigger and runtime-field options. Supplied empty strings and JSON objects are passed through. `workflow list` preserves explicitly supplied zero pagination, empty filters and `--include-dynamic-service=false`; omitted flags retain the previous request shape. Workflow `enable`, `pause` and `resume` send no request body in Go, Python and CLI.

The existing `versions`, `create-version`, `version`, `version-by-number`, `latest-published-version`, `publish-version`, `deprecate-version` and `delete-version` commands map directly to the eight Workflow Version routes. Candidate restore accepts `--parent-task-id` and `--parent-artifact-id`. `deploy-candidate` consumes the complete acceptance returned by `accept-candidate` and preserves explicitly supplied empty deployment options. Candidate submit/test keep `catalog_id` as a JSON number, including values above 2^53.

`workflow_versions_http_contract.json`, `workflow_apps_http_contract.json`, and `workflow_candidates_http_contract.json` compare the three public clients against local receivers. They cover raw and typed list results, update presence, version responses, acceptance/deployment handoff and owner errors; no real workflow is deployed or executed by these tests.

## Runner and sandbox HTTP alignment

Go `Client.Runners` / `Client.Sandboxes` and Python `client.runners` /
`client.sandboxes` use the current product handlers. CLI `runner` now exposes
create/list/get/refresh/delete. CLI `sandbox` exposes create/list/get/delete,
network updates, quota read/update, terminal-token, terminal connection checks and
SSH-session issuance, plus admin list/get/delete/network/quota operations. Typed body commands support
`--json` and `--json-file`; inspect `moi-cli schema sandbox COMMAND`.

Quota and admin inventory query options pass through with `--query-param`.
Admin quota uses `--target-workspace-id` for the explicit target path, distinct
from the request workspace header. Optional quota zero values retain presence;
omitted quota fields remain absent. Empty network lists follow the Go encoding
and owner's replacement semantics. Python exports `SandboxEgressEndpoint`,
`SandboxQuotaStatus` and `SandboxQuota` at the package root, matching Go's types.

`runner_sandbox_http_contract.json` covers the 19 HTTP routes in 30 shared
branches through Go/Python and the CLI dispatcher. It checks request scope,
method/path/query/body, empty inventory, one-time connection commands, partial
and zero quota updates, false network capability, admin target selection, exact
SSH expiry integers and simulated owner errors. Existing real Go coverage is
`TestAgentSandboxAndRunnerReadRoutes`, `TestAgentSandboxQuotaUpdateRoute`,
`TestAgentSandboxRunnerValidationRoutes`, `TestAgentSandboxOpenShellLifecycle`
and `TestAgentSandboxAdministrationProductSDK`; it has not been rerun here.

Terminal-token and SSH-session CLI commands only obtain credentials. The CLI
`connect-terminal` command uses a signed terminal token for an actual WebSocket
handshake, reports `{"connected":true}` and closes the probe; it is not an
interactive PTY. Go and Python `ConnectTerminal` / `connect_terminal` send stdin
as binary frames and resize as text JSON, and receive both binary output and
text `exit` / `error` control messages. Python close now releases immediately
instead of waiting three seconds when the peer does not acknowledge closure.
The local Go/Python/CLI terminal tests cover these wire behaviors; the existing
real Go `TestAgentSandboxTerminalWebSocketRoute` has not been rerun here. HTTP
parity alone does not establish a working terminal session. See the
[CLI environment procedures](../cli/skill/moi-cli/references/recipes/manage-compute-lifecycle.md).

## Workspace chat completion wire values

Go, Python and CLI share `workspace_chat_completion_contract.json` for ordinary
and streaming `WorkspaceChatCompletion`. The fixture includes nested message
content, a null tool and a function tool, explicit `tool_choice:null`, an empty
proxy object, zero-valued scalar options, a backend ID above `2^53`, and the
`service_slot` query. Zero scalars without presence stay omitted, the optional
backend ID remains a JSON integer, and streaming adds only `stream:true`.
Non-streaming returns the product data and streaming preserves the exact SSE
bytes.

The CLI previously decoded this request with `encoding/json`, which discarded
explicit null for the protobuf `Value` field. It now uses the existing shared
ProtoJSON decoder, matching Go and Python without adding a null-to-default or
repair rule. These local wire checks do not execute a model; existing Go model
route and provider scenarios remain the business acceptance layer.

## Workspace backend, endpoint and router wire values

Go, Python and CLI also share `workspace_model_http_contract.json` for workspace
LLM and Embedding backend CRUD, endpoint creation/listing, router read/update,
endpoint status changes, and workspace LLM model listing. Its 22 branches cover
21 routes, including backend and endpoint IDs above `2^53`, optional update
fields explicitly set to zero or empty strings, empty delete and LLM status
responses, the Embedding `updated:true` result, and owner `ErrConflict`
code/message/status passthrough. CLI JSON output is compared as the same typed
protobuf result as the SDKs, so omitted zero-value presentation fields are not
mistaken for a different response contract.

These are local HTTP contract checks, not provider or router execution tests.
The existing native acceptance scenarios remain in
`moi/tests/menu_workspace_model_routes_test.go` and
`moi/tests/menu_models_test.go`; they have not been rerun by this alignment
work.

## Provider backend and router options

CLI provider operations now pass the existing Go/Python provider options rather
than only a backend name. `provider-backend create` supports type, reasoning
protocol, credential file, address, timeout, models and parser MIME types;
`update` supports its corresponding update options. `update-router` exposes
strategy, health interval, retries and session affinity. `probe-backend-models`
uses the same credential-file input. `model setup-llm-backend`,
`probe-llm-models` and `probe-embedding-models` share the same option builders.

Only supplied flags create SDK options. Explicit empty strings, an empty
credential file, numeric zero and `session_affinity:false` retain presence;
omission stays absent. Integer flags parse at the SDK's int32 width. Credential
files are passed exactly and never included in successful output. The CLI does
not add empty-list clearing semantics absent from the typed SDK.

Nine local HTTP branches compare complete bodies for LLM and parser creation,
explicit empty update, full and empty router updates, backend probe, one-call LLM
setup and LLM/Embedding probes. They also check workspace/product
authentication and that provider secrets do not appear in output. Generated
help comes from the shared flag builders through skillgen. Real provider calls
and persistence remain with the existing Go integration scenarios and were not
run by this offline batch.

## AI Studio policy and service-slot administration

Go `AIStudioService`, Python `ai_studio()` and CLI `ai-studio` expose the same five
account-level routes: `chat-model-policy`, `service-slots`,
`service-slot-candidates`, `service-slot-logs` and `put-service-slot`. They never
send a workspace header. Model-policy read uses an ordinary product identity;
service-slot administration remains subject to the server's Genesis admin check.

Log `slot_id` and `page_size` are omitted unless requested. Put uses the existing
typed Go request, preserves `default_model_id`, `model_ids`, optional body
`slot_id` and `extra_json`, and sends `model_ids:[]` when the list is nil or empty.
An empty list clears the slot; it is not treated as an omitted update. The CLI
HTTP test repeats the Go/Python path escaping, query, complete bodies and owner
`ErrForbidden` contract, including an unfiltered log read. Existing native
`TestAIStudioServiceSlotAdministratorRoutes` remains the real business acceptance
and was not rerun by this offline check.

## Runtime gateway numeric and authorization contracts

`RuntimeGatewayService.ApplyComputeBindingEvent` / `apply_compute_binding_event`
preserve every event field and encode `fencing_token` as a JSON integer, including
values above `2^53`. The owner uses `encoding/json` into `int64`, so Python's
usual protobuf JSON string representation is not accepted here. Zero retains the
Go generated field's omission behavior; no alternate request or retry is used.
The CLI passes the same typed Go request.

`AuthorizeExecutor` / `authorize_executor` and the CLI `authorize-executor`
preserve all five task-scoped identifiers, use the runtime Bearer grant and accept
an empty HTTP 204 acknowledgement without manufacturing a result. These IDs do
not replace product authorization. `AgentService.ExecutorHealth` /
`executor_health` remains a product API-key request with workspace scope and
preserves optional `connected_secs` as an exact 64-bit value. The three local
contract tests assert the complete compute event and executor authorization
bodies, credentials and responses. The same three-client contract also covers
edge-token check/renewal, model resolution and ordinary MCP bodies,
and exact model SSE bytes with runtime Bearer authentication. Existing Go/Python
notification tests cover empty 202 responses and errors; the CLI now preserves
that empty acknowledgement instead of printing `null`.

These are local protocol checks, not real runtime admission or model execution.
The existing Go business scenarios are `TestRuntimeEdgeTokenCheckRoute`,
`TestRuntimeEdgeTokenRenewRoute`, `TestRuntimeModelResolveRoute`,
`TestRuntimeModelChatRoute`, `TestRuntimeMCPRoute`,
`TestRuntimeExecutorAuthorizeRoute`, `TestComputeBindingEventRoute`, plus the
Runner/Sandbox and terminal scenarios in `menu_agent_runtime_environments_test.go`.
They remain the native integration acceptance layer and were not rerun by these
offline tests.

## Structured error details

Go `Error.Details` now uses `ErrorDetails` (`map[string]any`), and Python
`Error.details` uses `dict[str, Any]`. The transport preserves the owner's nested
JSON object, including lists, booleans, null and empty collections. Go decodes
numbers as `json.Number`; Python retains integer values. Large resource IDs stay
exact through SDK error objects and CLI JSON/text output. The existing string
contracts for top-level `reason`, `domain` and `request_id` remain enforced.

This fixes the native dashboard source-conflict response: numeric business code
4, localized `message` and `details.tables` with affected chart identities now
reach callers. Previously both SDKs misclassified that valid object as
`ErrResponseInvalid`. SDK code remains a string as before; transport request IDs
remain separate from an owner's request ID inside details. Repair `data` is
preserved too.

Recognized Product envelopes whose details have a broken shape still return
`ErrResponseInvalid`, with the observed HTTP status and transport request ID,
including business-error envelopes received with HTTP 200. The CLI uses the SDK
error directly. A2A follow failures preserve all diagnostics and attach the task
being followed, replacing a stale task ID without changing the original error.

Go source callers must adapt the wider details value type: use `sdk.ErrorDetails`
or `map[string]any` in error literals, type-assert string values, and use the map's
presence result for optional keys. An absent key now reads as nil, rather than
the old string map's empty string. Existing string diagnostic values retain their
contents.

`sdk/testdata/product_error_details_contract.json` is shared by Go/Python and
CLI public command/error-output tests. Its branches cover the native conflict
shape, Product business rejection, typed diagnostics, repair data, flat/empty
details, download failures, malformed responses and task correlation. These are
local HTTP simulations; real Go product verification still uses the existing
integration suite.

## Dashboard request and result alignment

Go `DashboardService.PreviewSQL` accepts `WithDashboardPreviewChartID`, mirrored
by Python's `with_dashboard_preview_chart_id` and CLI `dashboard preview-sql
--chart-id`. The owner uses that chart's persisted database and SQL context;
omission retains the existing source-based preview. Explicit empty and omitted
selectors remain distinct request representations.

CLI `chart-result` and `download-chart-result` now accept the SDK's repeatable
`--query-param`, including the owner's `at=<RFC3339Nano>` historical result time.
The timestamp passes through without client conversion or fallback.

Python dashboard request encoding omits non-optional scalar defaults like Go.
Source table IDs stay exact JSON integers. Chart ordering deliberately sends a
`chart_ids` array, including `[]` for an empty dashboard: the owner rejects a
missing/null array. Go now preserves this required collection representation
instead of losing it to protobuf's ordinary `omitempty` encoding.

CLI drill-through preserves explicit protobuf `Value(null)`, sharing the decoder
with resource tags. The owner currently treats a missing drill-through value and
null as equivalent; this fixes request consistency, not a demonstrated backend
rejection. Numeric values are compared as exact JSON numbers (so `0` and `0.0`
agree), while booleans, strings, null and large integer IDs remain distinct.

`TaskExecutionOverviewResult` is now generated from the shared Product proto in
both SDKs, with the existing statistics plus the handler's `since` / `until`.
Python also exports this result type at the package root. CLI follows its usual
generated-model omission of zero-valued fields.

The shared `sdk/testdata/dashboard_http_contract.json` exercises the 18 ordinary
HTTP operations, including request omission, preview scope, pagination, exact
integers, zero statistics, CSV streams/files and simulated owner errors. Existing
business verification remains in `moi/tests/menu_data_dashboard_test.go` and
`menu_dashboard_test.go`; this batch has not executed them. The browser dashboard
WebSocket is a separate boundary, not covered by these HTTP comparisons. The
native contract explicitly records that Dashboard has no Product Go SDK
WebSocket entry point; browser protocol, heartbeat and disconnect behavior are
owned and tested by `moi-frontend`. This alignment therefore does not invent a
parallel SDK or CLI protocol. `TestDataDashboardWebSocketProductContract`
remains the native wire acceptance scenario and has not been rerun here.

## Resource tag CLI

`moi-cli resource-tag` now exposes the same twelve operations as Go's
`ResourceTagService` and Python's `resource_tags`: definitions, values, relation
updates, groups, resources and assignments. Lists expose their supported search,
resource and pagination flags. Body commands use the existing generated request
types through `--json` / `--json-file`; generated CLI schema and references
describe those types.

Tag updates preserve optional description presence, including an empty string.
Resource filters preserve `value_id: null` to select unassigned objects; a missing
selector still reaches the owner for validation. CLI protobuf JSON decoding
preserves this explicit null and keeps the ordinary CLI behavior of ignoring
unknown input fields. Authorization, validation and resource filtering stay in
the product service.

The shared `sdk/testdata/resource_tags_http_contract.json` compares requests and
typed responses across all three clients, including query encoding, omission,
null, relation changes, large integer response counts and simulated owner errors.
Real business verification reuses `TestResourceTags`,
`TestResourceTagAssignmentEditor` and `TestResourceTagsPermissions` in
`moi/tests/resource_tags_test.go`; this batch has not executed them.

## Persisted conversation requests

`ConversationService` manages persisted numeric session/message IDs and tags;
these APIs differ from Agent A2A tasks. Python now follows Go and CLI when
encoding their typed protobuf requests: non-optional scalar defaults and empty
repeated fields are omitted. Nonzero `session_id` / `after` values remain exact
JSON integers. The optional `expected_title` retains presence, including an
explicit empty string for conditional title updates.

The shared `sdk/testdata/conversation_http_contract.json` checks the twelve
body-bearing routes through public Go/Python/CLI calls, including pagination,
filters, large IDs, default values, conditional updates, tag attachment, empty
HTTP 204 acknowledgements and simulated owner error passthrough. It verifies
request alignment; existing Go product scenarios in `moi/tests/menu_agent_chat_test.go`
remain responsible for real persistence and business behavior.

## Knowledge runtime requests

The nine `/semantic-models/runtime/*` operations use API-key authentication and
also require the current live task's runtime authorization proof. Go's
`WithRuntimeAuthorization` and Python's `with_runtime_authorization` carry the
proof; prepared CLI conversations receive it from their command environment.
The server validates the task and resource scope. A plain API key does not
create a task authorization context.

Python now encodes the typed runtime request's `int64` model IDs as JSON numbers,
matching Go and CLI without losing integer precision. Optional zero IDs remain
present; omitted IDs remain absent, and cursor/content strings stay strings.
The handler accepts both protobuf JSON string and number representations of
these IDs; this change aligns the clients' requests rather than fixing a
confirmed backend rejection of string IDs.

CLI keyword search using flags retains its `max_hits=8`, `before=2`, `after=1`
defaults. Search with JSON input omits these implicit defaults, preserving SDK
semantics and the owner's source-page exclusion rules. Explicit flags remain
unless overridden by a field in JSON. Keyword JSON requests must supply their
intended `max_hits` and context bounds. Citation completion behavior is unchanged.

The shared `sdk/testdata/knowledge_runtime_http_contract.json` exercises all nine
routes through public Go/Python/CLI entry points. Its branches cover exact large
integers, optional zero/omission, nested local evidence, opaque strings, search
defaults, responses and simulated owner errors. These local HTTP checks do not
run a real task or replace the existing Go product integration suite.

## Knowledge creation controls

`KnowledgeService.CreateWithSpec` / `knowledge.create_with_spec` accept `SemanticModelCreateSpec` for the ordinary `POST /semantic-models` creation endpoint. The spec contains `name` and the creation-only controls `preserve_embedding`, `text_send_dimensions`, `image_send_dimensions`, `drawing_structured_enabled`, and `drawing_number_policy`. Description, tables and files continue to use the existing semantic-model options. `Create` / `create` use the same implementation with the controls omitted; update keeps its existing shared options.

An omitted drawing flag and explicit `false` have different meanings. Omission with no policy creates a standard knowledge base. Supplying the drawing flag, including `false`, or a non-empty policy requests a drawing knowledge base. `false` disables structured drawing tables; the server owns policy defaults and validation. `preserve_embedding=true` requires a complete text/image binding in files, including model, dimension and vector table; use it only with existing authoritative bindings.

Go:

```go
structured := false
created, err := workspace.Knowledge().CreateWithSpec(ctx,
    sdk.SemanticModelCreateSpec{Name: "drawings", DrawingStructuredEnabled: &structured},
    sdk.WithSemanticModelTables([]any{}))
```

Python:

```python
created = workspace.knowledge().create_with_spec(
    sdk.SemanticModelCreateSpec("drawings", drawing_structured_enabled=False),
    sdk.with_semantic_model_tables([]),
)
```

CLI:

```sh
moi-cli schema semantic-model create-with-spec
moi-cli semantic-model create-with-spec --json '{"name":"drawings","drawing_structured_enabled":false}' --tables-file "$tables_file"
```

`$tables_file` contains the intended semantic table definitions (`[]` for no initial tables). Optional `--description` and `--files-file` map to the same shared SDK options. Creation returns a model directly; `create-with-sources` returns its existing composite result. The existing `semantic-model create` / `create-empty` commands keep the dedicated empty-creation endpoint. The shared protocol fixture is `sdk/testdata/knowledge_create_spec_contract.json`; it checks omission, explicit false, policies, response fields and owner errors without starting a backend.

## Knowledge lifecycle and source governance

`KnowledgeService.ListSources` / `ListSourcesPage` and the corresponding Python methods accept the existing query options. Pass `include_display_status=true` to request the source display projection; `SemanticModelSourceListResult.display_status` preserves the returned value. CLI `list-sources` and `list-sources-page` expose `--include-display-status`. An omitted referencing-Agent limit stays omitted so the product default applies. CLI `check-source-existence` uses `--file-ids` and `--table-ids`; explicitly empty file ID flags produce `file_ids: []`, not `null`.

Drawing search preserves an explicitly supplied `top_k=0`. Go callers that need presence for zero set `DrawingSearchRequest.TopKPresent`; non-zero values retain their existing behavior. CLI accepts `--top-k=0` and `--query-image-filename` for multipart image search. Python keeps its existing optional `top_k` and image filename arguments. Source governance and segment commands retain explicit empty arrays, cleared expiry, `false`, empty content and `if_match`. Semantic-model validation sends no request body.

The shared offline contracts are `knowledge_lifecycle_http_contract.json`, `knowledge_governance_http_contract.json`, `knowledge_download_http_contract.json`, and `knowledge_runtime_http_contract.json`. They compare Go, Python and CLI requests, multipart fields, downloaded bytes, typed results and owner errors through local receivers; they do not start a real Knowledge backend.

## SQL filters and pagination

CLI SQL commands expose the existing Go/Python SDK options:

| Commands | Options |
| --- | --- |
| `sql workbooks` | `--search`, `--page-size`, `--page-token` |
| `sql workbook-versions` | `--page-size`, `--page-token` |
| `sql query-history-overview` | `--scope`, `--non-user`, `--start`, `--end`, `--sql-source-types` |
| `sql query-history` / `sql history` | Overview options plus `--status`, `--users`, `--databases`, `--statement`, `--query-types`, `--min-duration`, `--statement-id`, `--transaction-id`, `--session-id`, `--selected-fields`, `--offset`, `--limit`, `--order-by`, `--order`, `--cu` |
| `sql query-detail` / `sql query-profile` | `--scope`, `--start`, `--end` |
| `sql execute-query` | `--db-name`, `--sql-type`, `--offset`, `--limit` |
| `sql execute` / `sql run` | `--db`, `--sql-type`, `--offset`, `--limit`; existing `--query-file` remains available |
| `sql query-result` / `sql result` | `--offset`, `--limit` |

Only supplied flags become SDK options; explicit `false`, zero and empty strings retain the SDK's request semantics. List flags accept comma-separated values; empty lists follow Go's existing omission behavior. Cursor tokens are opaque: pass `next_page_token` back unchanged. History categories are `self` and `workspace`; empty scope follows Go's omission behavior in both SDKs. The server owns authorization for either category.

History overview/list times use RFC3339. If both are omitted, both SDKs supply their existing five-minute range; if one is supplied, they do not synthesize the other bound. Detail/profile keep the Go SDK's string time bounds. `sdk/testdata/sql_options_contract.json` supplies shared request/response examples for Go, Python and CLI; these local HTTP tests do not execute SQL against a real backend.

## SQL WebSocket protocol

Local Go and Python protocol tests exercise the same workspace-scoped handshake
and text frames: `ping` / `pong`, `query_execute` with explicit zero offset and
limit, `query_id`, `query_result` with a null value, and `query_kill`. Both SDKs
decode typed messages and close the connection. An idle Go read honors its
context deadline and an idle Python read honors its per-read timeout; both
sessions remain usable for the subsequent ping. Request credentials stay in the
handshake, while the negotiated protocol exposed to callers is the server's
non-secret `moi-api-key` marker.

CLI `sql connect-query-web-socket` performs that handshake, prints the selected
protocols and closes the probe. It is not an interactive SQL session; CLI query
execution continues to use execute/describe/result. The CLI regression checks
that the credential-bearing requested subprotocol is not copied to output and
that the server observes closure. These tests do not replace the existing real
`TestSQLEditorWebSocketHandshake` and SQL execution scenarios, which have not
been rerun by this batch.

## Compute configuration

Compute creation and updates support resource `kind`, Python worker requirements, replica bounds, GPU settings, worker images and platform through the existing option-based entry points. The server verifies the selected kind against the spec. CLI flags expose the same fields; worker selections use `--worker-images-file` with the schema shown by `moi-cli schema compute create --argument worker-images-file`.

| Setting | Go | Python | CLI |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Resource kind | `WithComputeKind`, `WithComputeUpdateKind` | `with_compute_kind`, `with_compute_update_kind` | `--kind` |
| Python requirements.txt contents | `WithComputePyRequirementsTxt`, `WithComputeUpdatePyRequirementsTxt` | `with_compute_py_requirements_txt`, `with_compute_update_py_requirements_txt` | `--py-requirements-txt` |
| Spec node placement | `WithComputeSpecNodePlacement`, `WithComputeSpecUpdateNodePlacement` | `with_compute_spec_node_placement` for both create and update | `--node-placement-file` |

Omitting requirements on an update retains the existing value; an explicit empty string clears them. Omitting node placement on a spec update retains it, while an explicit empty object or null clears it. SDKs send these values unchanged. The current spec-update owner retains existing scalar values when it receives zero, empty text or `enabled=false`; use `SetSpecEnabled` / `set_spec_enabled` / `compute set-spec-enabled` to disable an existing spec.

The shared response contract includes `py_requirements_txt`, `py_requirements_sha` and `py_deps` (`status`, `ready_count`, `total_count`, `last_error`). Dependency observations are optional: in Go check whether `GetPyDeps()` is nil; in Python use `HasField("py_deps")`. Absence means observations are unavailable in that response and does not imply that dependencies are disabled.

## Data Share publication and subscription wire values

Go, Python and CLI share `data_share_http_contract.json` across the twelve
currently mounted publication/subscription routes. Sixteen branches cover
publication and subscription filtering, membership-only and cross-user
workspace-name projection, summary/source-table counts above `2^53`, stable
string IDs, selected/all scopes, update presence, delete/unsubscribe empty
responses, explicit zero pagination rejection and owner conflict code/message/
status passthrough. CLI typed-output checks retain its existing void-write
success messages rather than inventing JSON result objects.

Create options now distinguish an omitted CLI `--remark` from an explicitly
empty remark. Python scope serialization follows Go protobuf JSON: empty
`object_ids` is omitted for `all`, while selected IDs remain an array. Neither
change adds scope repair, target defaults or client-side recovery. The second-
phase Volume routes described by the Data Share runbook are not currently
mounted and are not exposed prematurely by this batch. Existing native Data
Share lifecycle, permission and recovery scenarios remain the business
acceptance layer and have not been rerun here.

## User Story Matrix

AI callers should choose these promoted user-story entries before dropping to route-aligned services. Each row has Go and Python facade tests listed in `sdk/tests/user_story_facade_alignment_test.go`; adding a promoted row without both test anchors fails the SDK gate.

Not every backend capability belongs to the Product SDK. Agent resource CRUD/runtime routes remain route-aligned because the backend currently exposes them as `ANY` proxied resources rather than stable product contracts. Apps backend routes are `not_applicable` and must not be added to the User Story Matrix, route mappings, SDK facades, CLI, or smoke tests.

AI selection rule:

1. Classify the request as a product user task first, not as a backend route group.
2. Use the AI Intent Disambiguation Matrix when a request could match multiple promoted tasks or a route-aligned escape hatch; ask for explicit user choice if the source or outcome remains ambiguous.
3. Use the User Context Entry Matrix to choose the SDK starting object from the context already available; if required context is missing, ask for explicit user input or use the documented discover/create path before binding handles.
4. Check the User Story Ergonomic Usage Tiers before writing code; start with the minimal path, add advanced control only when the user asks or interaction mode requires it, and use escape boundaries only for uncovered route-level behavior.
5. Check the User Story Operation Intent Coverage before assuming a task supports a lifecycle action; each task must state its read/discover, create/configure, update/action, delete/cancel, and observe/result behavior or say the action is not promoted.
6. Check the User Story Capability Availability Coverage before choosing method names; the matched task must state availability probe, unavailable signal, required dependency/configuration, forbidden availability workaround, and AI-safe unavailable behavior.
7. Check the User Story Abstraction Fitness before choosing method names; each task must state the user mental model, friendly handle/spec shape, hidden route/scope details, caller-owned boundary, and forbidden low-level shortcut.
8. If the request spans multiple product areas, choose the Cross-Domain User Journeys outcome first, then follow the User Story Flow Recipes for each matched task before writing code; they define the ordered start point, next calls, produced handles/results, and source rules.
9. Choose the matching row in this matrix and start from its handle/spec entry points.
10. Check the User Story State Handoff Coverage before passing values between calls; each task must state carried typed state, public accessor/result source, next-call binding, forbidden handoff shortcut, and AI-safe handoff behavior.
11. Check the Cross-Domain Journey Example Anchors before copying README example lines for a multi-area outcome.
12. Check the Cross-Domain Journey Risk Coverage before composing retries, cancellations, reruns, permission-sensitive operations, or external dependency calls across product areas.
13. Check the User Story Discovery and Selection Coverage before constructing handles from ids; it identifies the promoted discover/create path and when route-aligned fallback is not a promoted discovery path.
14. Check the User Story Selection Disambiguation Coverage before selecting one resource from list, search, name, or paginated results; each task must state selection input, uniqueness rule, pagination/search boundary, ambiguous/missing selection handling, and forbidden selection shortcut.
15. Check the User Story Ordering and Recency Coverage before honoring latest, recent, oldest, first, top, sorted, priority, or time-window wording; each task must state ordering intent, authoritative order source, recency/time-window source, forbidden ordering shortcut, and AI-safe ordering behavior.
16. Follow the User Story Flow Recipe and User Story Outcome Contract before treating the task as complete; the outcome contract must name the user-visible result, continuation handle/spec, and caller-owned inputs or decisions.
17. Check the User Story Interaction Mode Coverage before writing code that polls, streams, paginates, batches, deletes, cancels, forces, or asks for approval.
18. Check the User Story Execution Budget Coverage before polling, streaming, paginating, retrying, or waiting on long-running work; each task must state timeout/deadline source, polling/stream stop condition, page/batch limit, retry budget, and approval/escalation boundary.
19. Check the User Story Pagination and Partial Result Coverage before treating first pages, previews, streams, batches, or capped reads as complete; each task must state paginated/partial surfaces, continuation token/source, complete-result proof, forbidden partial-result shortcut, and AI-safe pagination behavior.
20. Check the User Story Error Recovery Coverage before adding retries, reruns, prompts, or fallback behavior; each task must state retryable failures, user-correctable failures, forbidden recovery, and the AI-safe next action.
21. Check the User Story Permission and Scope Coverage before issuing permission-sensitive calls; each task must state product scope, permission-sensitive operations, caller-owned authorization boundary, forbidden scope/RBAC behavior, and AI-safe permission handling.
22. Check the User Story Ownership and Collaboration Coverage before modifying, exposing, deleting, sharing, or continuing work on resources in a shared workspace; each task must state ownership evidence, collaborator/shared scope, caller-owned decision boundary, forbidden cross-user shortcut, and AI-safe collaboration behavior.
23. Check the User Story Input Construction Coverage before constructing specs, options, enum values, secrets, or payloads; each task must state required caller inputs, derived typed-result inputs, option/enum/secret rules, forbidden construction behavior, and AI-safe construction.
24. Check the User Story Value Format and Units Coverage before serializing identifiers, timestamps, durations, byte sizes, limits, enum values, URLs, SQL, cron, or payload fields; each task must state value/format contract, unit/timezone authority, enum/identifier format, forbidden format shortcut, and AI-safe value behavior.
25. Check the User Story Default and Omission Coverage before relying on omitted fields, empty values, zero values, or backend defaults; each task must state optional omissions, invalid empty/zero values, backend/default authority, forbidden defaulting behavior, and AI-safe omission behavior.
26. Check the User Story Completion Verification Coverage before treating a write, async start, delete, cancel, or error-only method as complete; each task must state completion signal, verification call, async/delete/cancel handling, forbidden completion shortcut, and AI-safe stop condition.
27. Check the User Story Result Accessor Coverage before reading returned values; each task must state public result/accessor source, user-readable fields, continuation fields, forbidden extraction shortcut, and AI-safe accessor behavior.
28. Check the User Story Output Consumption Coverage before displaying or reusing returned values; each task must state user-facing output, continuation output, sensitive/internal output boundary, forbidden output consumption, and AI-safe consumption behavior.
29. Check the User Story Sensitive Data Handling Coverage before logging, persisting, showing examples, or passing SDK inputs/outputs into AI context; each task must state sensitive inputs, sensitive outputs, logging/example boundary, persistence/AI-context boundary, and AI-safe handling.
30. Check the User Story Automation Safety Coverage before repeating or automating SDK calls; each task must state repeat-safe behavior, idempotency key or lookup, external/cost side-effect boundary, forbidden automation shortcut, and AI-safe automation behavior.
31. Check the User Story Concurrent Submission Coverage before allowing parallel SDK calls, UI double-clicks, background polls, async retries, or multiple AI workers to act on the same task; the matched task must state operation identity, parallel-safe reads, serialized mutation boundary, forbidden duplicate submission, and AI-safe in-flight behavior.
32. Check the User Story Impact Assessment Coverage before asking for confirmation or executing high-impact SDK operations; each task must state impact source, affected scope, cost/external side effects, uncertainty boundary, and AI-safe impact summary.
33. Check the User Story Confirmation and Preview Coverage before destructive, cost-incurring, external, data-moving, permission-changing, or irreversible SDK operations; each task must state confirmation trigger, preview/preflight source, user-visible confirmation summary, forbidden implicit approval, and AI-safe confirmation behavior.
34. Check the User Story Abort and Cleanup Coverage before abandoning, canceling, compensating, or cleaning up partial SDK work; each task must state abort trigger, cleanup/compensation target, state proof before cleanup, forbidden rollback shortcut, and AI-safe abort behavior.
35. Check the User Story Progress Visibility Coverage before waiting on, streaming, paginating, batching, deleting, canceling, or otherwise monitoring SDK work; each task must state progress signal, status/read call, user-visible update, forbidden silent wait, and AI-safe progress reporting.
36. Check the User Story Diagnostic Handoff Coverage before asking a user, support engineer, or later AI step to debug a failed, stuck, or ambiguous task; each task must state safe diagnostic context, correlation/status handle, sensitive diagnostic boundary, forbidden diagnostic shortcut, and AI-safe diagnostic handoff.
37. Check the User Story Freshness and Conflict Coverage before updating, deleting, canceling, rerunning, publishing, or otherwise acting on previously read state; each task must state fresh-state source, conflict/stale signal, destructive/update conflict boundary, forbidden stale-state shortcut, and AI-safe conflict handling.
38. Check the User Story Stability Evidence for the matched task; it must list Go/Python facade tests, stable proof, and failure boundary before AI-written code treats the task as promoted.
39. Check the User Story Representative Method Coverage for the matched task; its Go/Python calls must be visible in language README examples before AI-written code treats the facade as user-friendly.
40. Check the User Story Example Scenario Coverage for the matched task; it must state user scenario, copyable example anchor, inputs to replace, expected user-visible output, and AI adaptation boundary before AI-written code adapts examples.
41. Bootstrap with the product endpoint and `EnsureWorkspace` / `ensure_workspace`; use `Workspace` / `workspace` only to bind an already sourced workspace id, then keep workspace-scoped operations on `WorkspaceHandle` descendants.
42. Use route-aligned services only as a documented escape hatch when no promoted row covers the task or when route-level control is explicitly required.
43. Do not expose Apps backend routes, or promote Agent resource proxy routes, raw request bodies, or `ProductData` responses as friendly SDK examples.
44. When a handle constructor needs an existing id, source that id from a typed SDK create/list/get result, a UI-selected resource, or explicit user input; do not invent ids, parse display labels, or infer ids from `ProductData`.
45. Treat error-only write methods as success/failure checks only. They do not return product data, ids, or handles, and AI-written code must not use them as the source for a later handle constructor or request field.

| User task | Go entry points | Python entry points | Notes |
|-----------|-----------------|---------------------|-------|
| Bootstrap a UI workspace and inspect identity | `Client.EnsureWorkspace`, `Client.Workspace`, `WorkspaceHandle` | `Client.ensure_workspace`, `Client.workspace`, `WorkspaceHandle` | Carries product auth and workspace scope through `moi-backend`; `Workspace` / `workspace` only binds an already sourced workspace id. |
| Manage workspace members | `WorkspaceMemberHandle`, `IAMService` | `WorkspaceMemberHandle`, `WorkspaceHandle` | The member handle carries profile/status/deletion scope; the workspace handle's `iam()` accessor carries canonical role-binding reads and replacements. |
| Browse catalog resources and operate tables | `CatalogHandle`, `DatabaseHandle`, `TableHandle`, `TableColumn` | `CatalogHandle`, `DatabaseHandle`, `TableHandle`, `TableColumn` | Use handles discovered from catalog/database children before table operations; table creation examples use typed columns. |
| Manage volumes, folders, and files | `VolumeHandle`, `FolderHandle`, `FileHandle`, `UploadedFilePreview`, `ConnectorFileHandle` | `VolumeHandle`, `FolderHandle`, `FileHandle`, `UploadedFilePreview`, `ConnectorFileHandle` | File and folder handles retain volume/database/catalog scope; preview helpers return the uploaded file handle plus preview link. |
| Deploy, run, inspect, rerun, and cancel workflows | `WorkflowHandle`, `WorkflowRunHandle`, `WorkflowRunNodeHandle`, `WorkflowRunRerunHandle` | `WorkflowHandle`, `WorkflowRunHandle`, `WorkflowRunNodeHandle`, `WorkflowRunRerunHandle` | Known workflow/execution/node ids must come from backend response, list, or UI state. |
| Design workflow custom operators and templates | `CustomOperatorHandle`, `CustomOperatorSpec`, `CustomOperatorTestInput`, `WorkflowTemplateHandle` | `CustomOperatorHandle`, `CustomOperatorSpec`, `CustomOperatorTestInput`, `WorkflowTemplateHandle` | Raw schema/payload paths remain escape hatches; common paths must use spec wrappers. |
| Publish and invoke WorkItem API services | `WorkitemAPIServiceHandle`, `WorkitemAPIServicePublishSpec`, `WorkitemAPIServiceInvokeSpec` | `WorkitemAPIServiceHandle`, `WorkitemAPIServicePublishSpec`, `WorkitemAPIServiceInvokeSpec` | Specs explicitly carry version; handles do not silently fill publish/invoke request fields. |
| Review lineage artifacts and rerun from nodes/blocks | `ArtifactHandle`, `ArtifactBlockHandle`, `ArtifactNodeHandle`, `ArtifactNodeBlockHandle`, `LineageRevisionSwitch` | `ArtifactHandle`, `ArtifactBlockHandle`, `ArtifactNodeHandle`, `ArtifactNodeBlockHandle`, `LineageRevisionSwitch` | Keeps artifact/node/block ids scoped to the workspace and artifact path; revision switches are explicit caller-reviewed inputs. |
| Manage semantic models and entries | `SemanticModelHandle`, `SemanticEntryHandle`, `SemanticEntryInput` | `SemanticModelHandle`, `SemanticEntryHandle`, `SemanticEntryInput` | Entry specs remain open JSON but are wrapped in explicit entry inputs. |
| Manage connectors plus import/export tasks | `ConnectorHandle`, `FileImportTaskSpec`, `ImportTaskUpdateSpec`, `ExportTaskSpec`, `ExportTaskConfig`, `ExportTaskFile`, `ImportTaskHandle`, `ExportTaskHandle` | `ConnectorHandle`, `FileImportTaskSpec`, `ImportTaskUpdateSpec`, `ExportTaskSpec`, `ExportTaskConfig`, `ExportTaskFile`, `ImportTaskHandle`, `ExportTaskHandle` | Prefer spec-based file import/update/export helpers over raw task route bodies. |
| Use product agents through A2A | `AgentHandle`, `AgentTextMessage`, `AgentTaskRequest`, `AgentInputSubmission` | `AgentHandle`, `AgentTextMessage`, `AgentTaskRequest`, `AgentInputSubmission` | Agent resource CRUD remains route-aligned until backend promotes stable product contracts. |
| Configure model providers and routers | `ModelProviderHandle`, `ProviderBackendHandle`, `ProviderRouterHandle` | `ModelProviderHandle`, `ProviderBackendHandle`, `ProviderRouterHandle` | Provider/backend/router handles keep provider family and backend id scoped. |
| Manage compute resources | `ComputeResourceHandle` | `ComputeResourceHandle` | Resource handles cover get/update/preflight/action/delete. |
| Retrieve consumer memories and render profiles | `ConsumerMemoryHandle` | `ConsumerMemoryHandle` | Handle performs semantic retrieval, typed keyword/metadata retrieval, typed structured queries, and profile rendering for one subject; subject id and query inputs stay caller-owned. |
| Publish and subscribe data shares | `DataSharePublishHandle`, `DataShareSubscriptionHandle` | `DataSharePublishHandle`, `DataShareSubscriptionHandle` | Publish/subscription handles keep ids explicit and scoped. |
| Work in the SQL editor and query history | `SQLMetadataHandle`, `SQLDatabaseMetadataHandle`, `SQLTableMetadataHandle`, `SQLWorkbookHandle`, `SQLWorkbookVersionHandle`, `SQLQueryHandle`, `SQLStatementHandle` | `SQLMetadataHandle`, `SQLDatabaseMetadataHandle`, `SQLTableMetadataHandle`, `SQLWorkbookHandle`, `SQLWorkbookVersionHandle`, `SQLQueryHandle`, `SQLStatementHandle` | Workbook/version/query/statement ids are explicit handles rather than inferred from raw `ProductData`. |

## Purpose

This document groups the Product SDK work by user-visible capability and records how each capability relates to `moi-core`.

Current Role selection is a typed workspace-scoped Product SDK capability. Go
callers configure mappings with `WithWorkspaceCurrentRoleID` when constructing a
client; `moi-cli` persists the same mappings with `config set-role` and reads one
snapshot per process. CLI Role changes therefore require a new `moi-cli` process;
there is no global one-shot Role override. Invalid persisted mappings fail closed
before business requests and can be repaired with `config show`, `config set-role`,
or `config unset-role`. HTTP final binding reserves `X-Workspace-ID` and
`X-MOI-Role-ID` after request modifiers and adds a Role only from the matching
typed mapping; generic extensions remain available for other headers. WebSocket
headers apply the same reserved binding after custom headers and have no HTTP
modifier phase. Credential-bearing
WebSocket header/subprotocol helpers are SDK-only in-process handshake inputs and
are intentionally not exposed as printable `moi-cli` commands.

This is a design document, not the CI source of truth for route coverage. Exact SDK coverage must be generated from the real `moi-backend` HTTP route and handler registration manifest, then compared with Go and Python SDK route mappings. A broad capability area can appear here without listing every route by hand.

### AI Decision Ladder

Use this ladder before the detailed coverage matrices. It is the shortest supported path from a user request to an SDK entry point, while the later tables provide the evidence and edge-case rules that make each step safe.

1. Classify the requested product outcome first; if it spans multiple product areas, choose the Cross-Domain User Journey before selecting individual task rows.
2. Resolve ambiguous wording with the AI Intent Disambiguation Matrix; ask for explicit user choice when the source or outcome remains unclear.
3. Choose the SDK starting object from the User Context Entry Matrix using only context already supplied by the user, typed SDK results, UI state, or documented discovery/create calls.
4. Select the promoted User Story Matrix row and its minimal handle/spec path from the User Story Ergonomic Usage Tiers; add advanced-control methods only when the user asks or the interaction mode requires them.
5. Before binding handles, verify every existing id against the Handle ID Source Matrix. Payloads, config, credentials, and external content must come from explicit caller input, UI state, or typed SDK/backend results.
6. Check only the detailed safety matrices needed by the selected lifecycle step: operation intent, availability, outcome, interaction mode, execution budget, pagination, recovery, permission/scope, ownership, input construction, value format, default/omission, completion, output, sensitive data, automation, concurrency, impact, confirmation, abort, progress, diagnostics, freshness, stability, and examples.
7. Use route-aligned services only when no promoted user-story facade covers the requested outcome or when explicit route-level control is required; document that choice and keep it out of friendly SDK examples.

### AI Task Selection Guide

Use this guide before selecting an SDK entry point from examples. The matched user task must already exist in the User Story Matrix. Prefer entries must be supported by the matching Ergonomic Usage Tiers minimal or advanced-control path, and each task must have at least one Prefer entry supported by the minimal path; otherwise stay on route-aligned services and document why no promoted task facade exists.

| User task | User intent signals | Prefer | Avoid |
|-----------|---------------------|--------|-------|
| Bootstrap a UI workspace and inspect identity | workspace bootstrap, known workspace id, DB connection, workspace-scoped setup | `Client.EnsureWorkspace`, `Client.Workspace`, `Client.ensure_workspace`, `Client.workspace`, `WorkspaceHandle` | Do not start with `client.Workspaces()` / `client.workspaces()` for promoted bootstrap flows; do not call `Workspace` / `workspace` unless the workspace id is already sourced. |
| Manage workspace members | Workspace membership, canonical role bindings, and member status. General audit access is available through `WorkspaceMemberService.AuditLogs`; the Product SDK has no member-scoped audit method. | `WorkspaceMemberHandle` for member lifecycle plus `IAMService` for role bindings | Do not use the removed Product SDK role, grant, or protected-object APIs; role and policy definitions are owned by canonical IAM. |
| Browse catalog resources and operate tables | catalog tree, database children, table create/info/data/preview/load/truncate/delete | `CatalogHandle`, `DatabaseHandle`, `TableHandle`, `TableColumn` | Do not jump to `client.Catalog(workspaceID)` / `client.catalog(workspace_id)` when catalog/database/table handles are available. |
| Manage volumes, folders, and files | upload, preview, download, folder lifecycle, volume files, data asset, file artifact | `VolumeHandle`, `FolderHandle`, `FileHandle`, `UploadedFilePreview`, `ConnectorFileHandle` | Do not manually repeat catalog/database/volume/file ids through route services for file lifecycle tasks. |
| Deploy, run, inspect, rerun, and cancel workflows | deploy workflow, run workflow, execution result, node detail, rerun, retry, cancel | `WorkflowHandle`, `WorkflowRunHandle`, `WorkflowRunNodeHandle`, `WorkflowRunRerunHandle` | Do not call `client.Workflows(workspaceID)` / `client.workflows(workspace_id)` first for promoted workflow lifecycle flows. |
| Design workflow custom operators and templates | custom operator, operator test, workflow template, code/schema for workflow design | `CustomOperatorHandle`, `CustomOperatorSpec`, `CustomOperatorTestInput`, `WorkflowTemplateHandle` | Do not use raw schema/payload maps when spec wrappers cover the common path. |
| Publish and invoke WorkItem API services | WorkItem API publish, enable/disable API service, invoke one catalog WorkItem version | `WorkitemAPIServiceHandle`, `WorkitemAPIServicePublishSpec`, `WorkitemAPIServiceInvokeSpec` | Do not silently fill publish/invoke fields or bypass the versioned WorkItem API service handle. |
| Review lineage artifacts and rerun from nodes/blocks | lineage, artifact overview, output blocks, revisions, rerun from artifact node/block | `ArtifactHandle`, `ArtifactBlockHandle`, `ArtifactNodeHandle`, `ArtifactNodeBlockHandle` | Do not parse artifact/node/block ids from rendered labels or raw lineage `ProductData`. |
| Manage semantic models and entries | semantic model lifecycle, entry list/create/update/delete, import/export, validate | `SemanticModelHandle`, `SemanticEntryHandle`, `SemanticEntryInput` | Do not infer model or entry ids from display names or untyped result blobs. |
| Manage connectors plus import/export tasks | connector lifecycle, source DB/schema/table browse, import task, export task, connector file | `ConnectorHandle`, `FileImportTaskSpec`, `ImportTaskUpdateSpec`, `ExportTaskSpec`, `ExportTaskConfig`, `ExportTaskFile`, `ImportTaskHandle`, `ExportTaskHandle` | Do not hand-build raw import/export task route bodies when specs exist. |
| Use product agents through A2A | agent card, send message, stream message, task get/cancel/resubscribe, submit input | `AgentHandle`, `AgentTextMessage`, `AgentTaskRequest`, `AgentInputSubmission` | Do not promote Agent resource CRUD/runtime proxy routes as friendly SDK examples. |
| Configure model providers and routers | LLM/embedding/parser backend setup, endpoints, model probing, router strategy | `ModelProviderHandle`, `ProviderBackendHandle`, `ProviderRouterHandle` | Do not write provider secrets directly to core or bypass product provider handles. |
| Manage compute resources | compute create/get/update/delete, preflight, suspend/resume/retry | `ComputeResourceHandle` | Do not infer compute resource ids from worker image names or runtime labels. |
| Publish and subscribe data shares | data share publish, update/delete publication, subscribe/unsubscribe, share summary | `DataSharePublishHandle`, `DataShareSubscriptionHandle` | Do not invent publish/subscription/target workspace/object ids. |
| Work in the SQL editor and query history | SQL metadata, workbook/version editing, execute query, query history, statement result/profile | `SQLMetadataHandle`, `SQLDatabaseMetadataHandle`, `SQLTableMetadataHandle`, `SQLWorkbookHandle`, `SQLWorkbookVersionHandle`, `SQLQueryHandle`, `SQLStatementHandle` | Do not derive workbook/query/statement ids from SQL text or display labels. |

### AI Intent Disambiguation Matrix

Use this matrix when a user request could match multiple promoted tasks or a route-aligned escape hatch. Do not choose by route or service name alone. Pick the row whose user-visible outcome and source rule match the request. If the source or outcome is still ambiguous, ask for explicit user choice before selecting an SDK entry point.

| Ambiguous request | Choose this task when | Do not choose | Required source question |
|-------------------|-----------------------|---------------|--------------------------|
| workflow rerun vs lineage rerun | Choose `Deploy, run, inspect, rerun, and cancel workflows` when the user references workflow execution, run, node detail, retry, cancel, or rerun branch operations. | Do not choose `Review lineage artifacts and rerun from nodes/blocks` unless the user references artifacts, output blocks, revisions, effective revisions, or artifact node/block rerun. | Ask whether the rerun target is a workflow run node or a lineage artifact revision/block. |
| file upload vs connector import | Choose `Manage volumes, folders, and files` when the user wants upload, preview, download, folder lifecycle, or volume file artifact operations. | Do not choose `Manage connectors plus import/export tasks` unless the user references connector configuration, external source browsing, import task, export task, credentials, or storage config. | Ask whether the source is a local/volume file action or a connector import/export task. |
| Agent A2A vs Agent resource proxy | Choose `Use product agents through A2A` when the user wants agent card, send/stream message, task get/cancel/resubscribe, or input-submit behavior. | Do not choose the Agent resource CRUD/runtime route-aligned escape hatch unless route-level resource control is explicitly required. | Ask whether the desired outcome is an A2A conversation/task or route-level agent resource/runtime control. |
| model provider vs semantic model | Choose `Configure model providers and routers` when the user references LLM, embedding, parser backend, endpoints, probing, router, or provider secrets. | Do not choose `Manage semantic models and entries` unless the user means business semantic model lifecycle, entries, table/file bindings, import/export, or validation. | Ask whether the target is provider/router infrastructure or a semantic model definition. |
| RBAC vs data share | Choose `Manage workspace members` when the user references users, roles, permission grants, protected objects, or permission audit. | Do not choose `Publish and subscribe data shares` unless the user wants publication/subscription, share summary, target workspace, or shared data object operations. | Ask whether the goal is access control or data publication/subscription. |
| compute action vs workflow/provider action | Choose `Manage compute resources` when the user references compute resource create/get/update/delete, preflight, suspend/resume/retry, or worker specs. | Do not choose `Deploy, run, inspect, rerun, and cancel workflows` or `Configure model providers and routers` unless the action target is a workflow run/node or provider backend/endpoint/router. | Ask whether the action target is a compute resource, workflow execution, or provider backend. |
| Apps backend route vs Product SDK facade | Treat Apps routes as backend/UI-owned and Product SDK `not_applicable`. | Do not add Apps DTOs, facades, route mappings, CLI commands, or smoke tests. | Direct the request to the backend/UI owner rather than inventing a Product SDK entry. |

### User Context Entry Matrix

Use this matrix after intent disambiguation and before the User Story Flow Recipes to choose the SDK starting object from the context the user or AI already has. Do not silently upgrade, downgrade, normalize, or invent context. If the required context is missing, ask for explicit user input or use the documented discover/create path before binding handles.

| Available context | Start from | Continue with | Do not |
|-------------------|------------|---------------|--------|
| Product endpoint plus API key/session and workspace name | `sdk.New` / `sdk.Client`, then `Client.EnsureWorkspace` / `client.ensure_workspace`; call `DBConnectionResult` / `db_connection` when identity is needed. | `WorkspaceHandle` from ensure result, then the matched User Story Flow Recipe. | Do not call `Workspace` / `workspace` without an already sourced workspace id, and do not derive workspace id from workspace name or endpoint. |
| Already sourced workspace id | `Client.Workspace` / `client.workspace` to bind the existing workspace. | `WorkspaceHandle` descendants for the promoted task. | Do not treat workspace display name, account name, or endpoint path as a workspace id; do not derive or invent workspace ids. |
| Typed create/list/get result | The handle returned by the facade or a handle constructor fed by ids from that typed result. | The next handle or spec named by the Flow Recipe `Produces` and Outcome Contract `Continue with` cells. | Do not parse ids from display labels, rendered paths, log text, SQL text, URLs, or raw `ProductData`. |
| UI-selected resource ids | The relevant handle constructor only after the UI selection provides the exact id/key/code/version fields required by the handle. | The matched handle operation, with caller-owned decisions kept explicit. | Do not normalize, trim, remap, or fill missing selected ids; invalid or incomplete selection must fail or ask the user. |
